Density of weak solutions of the fractional Navier-Stokes equations in the smooth divergence-free vector fields
Michele Gorini

Abstract
In this paper, we consider the fractional Navier-Stokes equations. We extend a previous non-uniqueness result due to Cheskidov and Luo, found in [ 3 (C-L) ] , from Navier-Stokes to the fractional case, and from L 1 L^{1} -in-time, W 1 , q W^{1,q} -in-space solutions for every q > 1 q>1 to L s L^{s} -in-time, W 1 , q W^{1,q} -in-space solutions for appropriate ranges of s , q s,q .



Keywords:
Convex Integration
,
fractional Navier-Stokes equations
,
non-uniqueness

1
Introduction

1.1
Background

We consider the fractional Navier-Stokes equations on the
d
-dimensional torus
\mathbb{T}^{d}=[-\pi,\pi]^{d}
:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\partial_{t}v+\operatorname{div}(v\otimes v)+\nabla p+(-\Deltaup)^{\theta}v=0\\
\operatorname{div}v=0\end{array}\right.,

where
v=(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})^{T}(t,x)\!\in\!\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the velocity and
p(t,x)\!\in\!\mathbb{R}
is the pressure of the fluid, and the fractional laplacian, for
\theta\geq 0
, is defined vie the Fourier transform:

(-\Deltaup)^{\theta}u(t,k)=|k|^{2\theta}\hat{u}(t,k).

For
\theta=1
, these are the Navier-Stokes equations, a fundamental mathematical model describing the motion of an incompressible viscous Newtonian fluid. The fractional case
\theta\neq 1
, for suitable exponents (
\theta\!\in\![\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}},1]
), can also be used to model fluid flow, as described in
[
62 (FNS-Fis)
]
.

The corresponding model for inviscid fluids is given by the Euler equations:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\partial_{t}v+\operatorname{div}(v\otimes v)+\nabla p=0\\
\operatorname{div}v=0\end{array}\right..

We consider weak solutions defined as space-time distributional solutions.

Definition 1.1.1
(name=Weak solutions).

Let
\mathcal{D}_{T}
be the space of divergence-free test functions
\phi\!\in\!\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{d})
such that
\phi=0
if
t\geq T
. Let
u_{0}\!\in\!L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
be weakly divergence-free. A vector field
u\!\in\!L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{x}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})
is a
weak solution of (
1.1
) with initial data
u_{0}
if the following hold:

1)

For a.e.
t\!\in\![0,T]
,
u(t,\cdot)
is weakly divergence-free;

2)

For any
\phi\!\in\!\mathcal{D}_{T}
,

\int\limits_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\mskip 4.0mu}^{\mskip 4.0mu}\hskip-2.0ptu_{0}(x)\cdot\phi(0,x)\mskip 0.0mu\mathrm{d}\mskip 0.5mux=-\int\limits_{0\mskip 4.0mu}^{\mskip 4.0muT}\int\limits_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\mskip 4.0mu}^{\mskip 4.0mu}\hskip-2.0ptu\cdot(\partial_{t}\phi-(-\Deltaup)^{\theta}\phi+u\cdot\nabla\phi)\mskip 0.0mu\mathrm{d}\mskip 0.5mux\mskip 0.0mu\mathrm{d}\mskip 0.5mut.

Weak solutions for the Euler equations (
1.1
)
are defined similarly, by removing the term
u\cdot(-\Deltaup)^{\theta}\phi
from the integral in the formula above.

A more physical class of solutions, which Leray introduced in the case
\theta=1
and proved to exist in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
in
[
57 (L)
]
, and Hopf proved to exist in general domains in
d\geq 2
for
\theta=1
, is that of so-called “admissible” or “Leray-Hopf” weak solutions, which satisfy an energy inequality.

Definition 1.1.2
(name=Admissible solutions).

A weak solution of (
1.1
) is called an
admissible weak solution
if
u\!\in\!\mathcal{C}_{w}([0,T];L^{2})\cap L^{2}_{t}H^{\theta}_{x}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})
and

\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\left\|u(t)\right\|}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}^{2}+\int\limits_{0\mskip 4.0mu}^{\mskip 4.0mut}{\left\|(-\Deltaup)^{\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\theta$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle\theta$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\theta$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\theta$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}}u(s)\right\|}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}^{2}\mskip 0.0mu\mathrm{d}\mskip 0.5mus\leq\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\left\|u(0)\right\|}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}^{2}

for all
t\!\in\![0,T]
.

A similar definition is given for (
1.1
), once again suppressing the
(-\Deltaup)^{\theta}
term from the energy inequality, and not requiring the
L^{2}_{t}H^{\theta}_{x}
regularity.

Remark 1.1.1
(name=Euler and Leray).

The term “Leray-Hopf weak solution” is not used for the Euler equations because there is no existence result for admissible solutions of the Euler equations with generic
L^{2}
initial data.

This energy inequality, also called admissibility condition, is a relaxation of the natural conservation law of (
1.1
) (or (
1.1
)). Solutions which satisfy it enjoy much better properties than general weak solutions. At least in the case
\theta=1
, there is a vast literature on the topic, giving results such as weak-strong uniqueness
[
68 (P)
,
73 (S)
,
55 (La)
]
and partial regularity
[
55 (La)
,
74 (S2)
]
. For more such properties, cfr.
[
3 (C-L)
,
6 (L-Q-Z-Zh)
]
and the references therein. Even in the fractional case, it is relatively simple to prove weak-strong uniqueness for
\mathcal{C}^{1}
solutions.

Such properties, though nice from a regularity point of view, make it very hard to construct non-unique Leray-Hopf weak solutions in
d\geq 3
(
1
1

1

2D solutions in the case
\theta=1
are known to be unique and smooth.
). Indeed, the problem of uniqueness (or non-uniqueness) of Leray-Hopf weak solutions remains a challenging open one. So far, there are only numerical evidence
[
6 (N-E-N-U)
]
and partial results.

One direction of these partial results is to work with forced Navier-Stokes, namely the following system:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\partial_{t}v+\operatorname{div}(v\otimes v)+\nabla p+(-\Deltaup)^{\theta}v=f\!\in\!L^{1}_{t}L^{2}_{x}\\
\operatorname{div}v=0\end{array}\right.,

with admissibility condition

\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\left\|u(t)\right\|}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}^{2}+\int\limits_{0\mskip 4.0mu}^{\mskip 4.0mut}{\left\|(-\Deltaup)^{\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\theta$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle\theta$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\theta$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\theta$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}}u(s)\right\|}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}^{2}\mskip 0.0mu\mathrm{d}\mskip 0.5mus\leq\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\left\|u(0)\right\|}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}^{2}+\int\limits_{0\mskip 4.0mu}^{\mskip 4.0mut}\int\limits_{\mathbb{T}^{3}\mskip 4.0mu}^{\mskip 4.0mu}\hskip-2.0ptf\cdot v\mskip 0.0mu\mathrm{d}\mskip 0.5mux\mskip 0.0mu\mathrm{d}\mskip 0.5mus

A non-uniqueness result for Leray-Hopf weak solutions of (
1.1
), for a suitable forcing term
f
, was proved in
[
1 (A-B-C)
]
.

The other direction is to consider general solutions which may or may not have sufficient regularity to be tested for admissibility. The first result in this direction is
[
2 (B-V)
, Theorem 1.2]
.

Theorem 1.1.1
(name=Buckmaster-Vicol,label=thm:teor:BV).

There exists
\beta>0
such that, for any non-negative smooth function
e(t):[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, there exists
v\!\in\!\mathcal{C}^{0}_{t}H^{\beta}_{x}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{3})
a weak solution of the Navier-Stokes equations such that, for all
t\!\in\![0,T]
,

e(t)=\int\limits_{\mathbb{T}^{3}\mskip 4.0mu}^{\mskip 4.0mu}\hskip-2.0pt{\left|v(t,x)\right|}^{2}\mskip 0.0mu\mathrm{d}\mskip 0.5mux.

Moreover, the associated vorticity
\nabla\times v
lies in
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{t}L^{1}_{x}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{3})
.

The result of
[
2 (B-V)
]
immediately yields non-uniqueness for zero initial data, but nontrivial solutions with zero initial data cannot be admissible, since the kinetic energy itself has to increase.

A similar result was obtained for the Euler equations (
1.1
) in
[
9 (B-DL-Sz-V)
]
for
\mathcal{C}^{\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 3$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 3$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$3$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$3$}}$}}-}
solutions.

Theorem 1.1.2
(name=Buckmaster-De Lellis-Székelyhidi-Vicol,label=thm:teor:BDLSzV).

For every
\beta<{}^{1}\!/_{3}
and every positive smooth
E:[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
, there exists a solution
(v,p)\!\in\!\mathcal{C}^{\beta}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{3})
of the Euler equations such that

\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}\int\limits_{\mathbb{T}^{3}\mskip 4.0mu}^{\mskip 4.0mu}\hskip-2.0pt{\left|v(t,x)\right|}^{2}\mskip 0.0mu\mathrm{d}\mskip 0.5mux=E(t).

This was in the context of the Onsager conjecture (formulated in
[
67 (O)
]
by Lars Onsager), which states that admissible solutions of the Euler equations (i.e. which satisfy the energy inequality for those equations, which reads
\|u(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}}\leq\|u(0,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}}
) are unique in
\mathcal{C}^{\beta}
for
\beta>\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 3$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 3$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$3$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$3$}}$}}
, whereas non-uniqueness holds for
\beta<\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 3$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 3$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$3$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$3$}}$}}
.

The presence of the Laplacian term in the fractional Navier-Stokes equations is an obstacle in proving non-uniqueness of Leray-Hopf weak solutions, as it proves hard to control. However, if the exponent is small enough (
\theta<\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 3$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 3$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$3$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$3$}}$}}
), non-uniqueness can still be proved in a very strong fashion (cfr.
[
18 (C-DL-DR)
,
30 (DR)
,
40 (G)
]
). For
\theta\geq\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 3$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 3$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$3$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$3$}}$}}
, we are led back to general solutions.

Another non-uniqueness result was proved in
[
1 (B-C-V)
]
, both for
\theta=1
and for some fractional values of
\theta
. The authors of the paper first proved the following gluing theorem, which is
[
1 (B-C-V)
, Theorem 1.5]
, and from there deduced the non-uniqueness of
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{t}H^{\beta}_{x}
weak solutions of (
1.1
), for any
\alpha\!\in\![1,\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 5$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 4$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 5$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 4$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$5$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$4$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$5$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$4$}}$}})
, any initial datum
v_{0}\!\in\!\dot{H}^{3}
, and any sufficiently small
\beta
.

Theorem 1.1.3
(name=Buckmaster-Colombo-Vicol,label=thm:teor:BCV).

For
\theta\!\in\![1,\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 5$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 4$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 5$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 4$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$5$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$4$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$5$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$4$}}$}})
, there exists a
\beta=\beta(\theta)>0
(
2
2

2

The maximal
\beta_{max}(1)
for which this holds in the case
\theta=1
can be quantified as
\beta_{max}\approx 10^{-3}
.
) such that the following holds. For
T>0
, let
v^{(1)},v^{(2)}\!\in\!\mathcal{C}^{0}([0,T];\dot{H}^{3}(\mathbb{T}^{3})\!)
be two strong solutions of the Navier-Stokes equations (
1.1
) on
[0,T]
, with data
v^{(1)}(0,x)
and
v^{(2)}(0,x)
of zero mean. There exists a weak solution
\overline{v}
of the Cauchy problem to (
1.1
) on
[0,T]
with initial datum
\overline{v}|_{t=0}=u^{(1)}|_{t=0}
, which has the additional regularity
\overline{v}\!\in\!\mathcal{C}^{0}([0,T];H^{\beta}(\mathbb{T}^{3})\cap W^{1,1+\beta}(\mathbb{T}^{3})\!)
, and such that
\overline{v}\equiv v^{(1)}
on
[0,{}^{T}\!/{\!}_{3}]
and
\overline{v}\equiv v^{(2)}
on
[{}^{2}\!/{\!}_{3}T,T]
. Moreover, for every such
\overline{v}
, there exists a zero Lebesgue measure set of times
\Sigmaup_{T}\!\subset\![0,T]
with Hausdorff (in fact box-counting) dimension less than
1-\beta
such that
\overline{v}\!\in\!\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\!(\!(0,T]\smallsetminus\Sigmaup_{T})\times\mathbb{T}^{3})
. In particular,
\overline{v}
is smooth almost everywhere.

Returning to
\theta=1
, in
[
3 (C-L)
]
, Cheskidov and Luo prove non-uniqueness for
L^{1}_{t}W^{1,q}_{x}
solutions for any
q
. They do this via a density theorem,
[
3 (C-L)
, Theorem 1.7]
, which I will frame as a “meta-theorem” plus a choice of parameter ranges.

Theorem 1.1.4
(name=Meta-theorem).

Let
d\geq 2
be the dimension,
\theta>0
,
1\leq p<2,q,s<\infty
, and
\gamma,\epsilon>0
. For any smooth, divergence-free vector field
v\!\in\!\mathcal{C}^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})
with zero spatial mean for each
t\!\in\![0,T]
, henceforth known as “starting field”, there exists a weak solution
u
of (
1.1
) and a set:

\mathcal{I}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}(a_{i},b_{i})\!\subset\![0,T],

such that the following holds.

(1)

The solution u satisfies
u\!\in\!L^{p}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})\cap L^{s}_{t}W^{\gamma,q}_{x}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})
;

(2)

u
is a smooth solution on
(a_{i},b_{i})
for every
i
. Namely,
u|_{\mathcal{I}\times\mathbb{T}^{d}}\!\in\!\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{I}\times\mathbb{T}^{d})
. In addition,
u
agrees with the smooth solution emerging from the initial data
v(0)
near
t=0
; in fact, if there is an interval
[a,b]\!\subseteq\![0,T]
where
v
is an exact solution, then
u=v
on
[a,b]
;

(3)

The Hausdorff dimension of the residue set
\mathcal{S}=[0,T]\smallsetminus\mathcal{I}
satisfies
d_{\mathcal{H}}\mathcal{S})\leq\epsilon
;

(4)

The solution
u
and the starting field
v
are
\epsilon
-close in
L^{p}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}\cap L^{1}_{t}W^{1,q}_{x}
.

Taking any smooth datum
v_{0}
, one can consider the smooth solution
\tilde{v}
with that initial datum, and glue it to a different smooth field
u
by use of a cutoff function:

\tilde{u}\coloneq\chi\tilde{v}+(1-\chi)u,

where
\chi
is 1 near
t=0
and
\chi=1
near
t=T
. By applying the meta-theorem to
\tilde{u}
, one quickly concludes both non-uniqueness of solutions and a gluing theorem à la
[
1 (B-C-V)
]
for the ranges where the meta-theorem can be proved to hold.

In this framing,
[
3 (C-L)
, Theorem 1.7]
can be stated as follows.

Theorem 1.1.5
(name=Cheskidov-Luo,label=thm:teor:CL).

The meta-theorem holds for
\theta=1,s=1,\gamma=0
, and any
q
.

Compared to
[
1 (B-C-V)
]
, this suggests there may be a tradeoff between space regularity and time regularity: giving up some time regularity (
\mathcal{C}^{0}
in
[
1 (B-C-V)
]
vs.
L^{1}
in
[
3 (C-L)
]
), we have gained space regularity (
H^{\beta}
with small
\beta
in
[
1 (B-C-V)
]
vs.
W^{1,q}
for any
q
in
[
3 (C-L)
]
).

This result was then extended to
L^{s}_{t}W^{\gamma,q}_{x}
and
\theta\neq 1
in
[
6 (L-Q-Z-Zh)
, Theorem 1.2]
, which can be restated as follows.

Theorem 1.1.6
(name=Li-Qu-Zong-Zhang,label=thm:teor:LQZZh).

The meta-theorem holds whenever one of the following holds:

\displaystyle(\theta,\gamma,s,q)\!\in\!{}

\displaystyle{\left[\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 5$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 4$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 5$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 4$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$5$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$4$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$5$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$4$}}$}},2\right)}\times[0,3)\times[0,\infty]\times[1,\infty]\quad 0\leq\gamma<\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 4\theta-5$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 4\theta-5$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$4\theta-5$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$s$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$4\theta-5$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$s$}}$}}+\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 3$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle q$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 3$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle q$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$3$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$q$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$3$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$q$}}$}}+1-2\theta

\displaystyle(\theta,\gamma,s,q)\!\in\!{}

\displaystyle[1,2)\times[0,3)\times[1,\infty]\times[1,\infty]\quad 0\leq\gamma<\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2\theta$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2\theta$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$2\theta$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$s$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$2\theta$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$s$}}$}}+\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2\theta-2$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle q$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2\theta-2$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle q$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$2\theta-2$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$q$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$2\theta-2$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$q$}}$}}+1-2\theta.

The solutions can also be chosen in
H^{\beta^{\prime}}_{t,x}
for sufficiently small
\beta^{\prime}<1
, and with supports close to that of the starting field.

1.2
Main theorem

This paper is devoted to proving the following theorem and corollary.

Theorem 1.2.1
(name=,label=thm:teor:Relaz).

Let
d\geq 2
be the dimension,
\epsilon>0
, and
p,q,s,\beta^{\prime},\delta^{\prime},\zeta^{\prime},\gamma,\theta
satisfy the following system:

\displaystyle\beta^{\prime},\delta^{\prime},\zeta^{\prime}>{}

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle p,s\geq{}

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle q>{}

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle\delta^{\prime}<{}

\displaystyle 2\beta^{\prime}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle p$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2-p$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle p$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2-p$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$p$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2-p$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$p$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2-p$}}$}}(d-1)<{}

\displaystyle\zeta^{\prime}

\displaystyle s{\left(\delta^{\prime}\gamma+\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\zeta^{\prime}$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle\zeta^{\prime}$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\zeta^{\prime}$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\zeta^{\prime}$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}+\gamma+\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$d-1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$d-1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}-\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle q$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle q$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$d-1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$q$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$d-1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$q$}}$}}\right)}<{}

\displaystyle\zeta^{\prime}

\displaystyle 2\beta^{\prime}+\zeta^{\prime}<{}

\displaystyle 2\delta^{\prime}+d+1

\displaystyle 2\theta\leq{}

\displaystyle\gamma+1

\displaystyle\gamma\leq{}

\displaystyle d

\displaystyle q\leq{}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2d$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2\gamma-d$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2d$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2\gamma-d$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$2d$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2\gamma-d$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$2d$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2\gamma-d$}}$}}\bb{1}_{2\gamma>d}+\infty\bb{1}_{2\gamma\leq d}.

Then, the meta-theorem holds for
p,q,s,\theta
.

By studying the system, one can arrive at the following definitions and corollary.

\displaystyle Q(d,\gamma)\coloneq{}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\gamma-1$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle\gamma-1$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$d-1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\gamma-1$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$d-1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\gamma-1$}}$}}

Corollary 1.2.1

;

.

The paper is organized as follows. In section
2
, we give an outline of the proof, stating the main iteration proposition, which lies at the heart of the proof, at the end of the section. In section
3
, we carry out the first substep of the convex integration step, which is a gluing argument. In section
4
, we show how the second substep is done, namely the perturbation step. All three of these sections are very similar to the corresponding sections of
[
3 (C-L)
]
, so the various lemmas and propositions are mostly left without proof, as the proofs can be found in
[
3 (C-L)
]
. In section
5
, we show that, given a set of relations between the parameters of the convex integration step, we can obtain the satisfactory estimates that lead to the proof of the meta-theorem. This section is also quite similar to the corresponding one in
[
3 (C-L)
]
, except that, instead of choosing the parameters first and immediately deducing the needed relations, we postpone the choice of the parameters to section
6
. In that last section, we gather the relations, adding an extra couple which arise in the gluing argument of section
3
, and study the resulting system, completing the proofs of
(??)
and
(??)
.

2
Outline of the proof

The proof of
(??)
consists of an iterative scheme achieved by a repeated application of this paper’s main proposition,
(??)
, to obtain a sequence of solutions to the fractional Navier-Stokes-Reynolds system below:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\partial_{t}v+\operatorname{div}(v\otimes v)+\nabla p+(-\Deltaup)^{\theta}v=-\operatorname{div}R\\
\operatorname{div}v=0\end{array}\right...

We are omitting the pressure when referring to solutions of (
2
) because it can be uniquely determined by the following elliptic equation, provided it is average-free:

\mathbb{D}p=\operatorname{div}\operatorname{div}R-\operatorname{div}\operatorname{div}(v\otimes v).

The proof mainly consists of three goals:

(a)

The convergence of
u_{n}\to u
in
L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{x}
and of
R_{n}\to 0
in
L^{1}_{t}L^{1}_{x}
so that
u
is a weak solutions of (
1.1
);

(b)

The convergence of
u_{n}\to u
in
L^{p}_{t}L^{q}_{x}\cap L^{s}_{t}W^{\gamma,q}_{x}
;

(c)

The small dimension of the set of the singular times of
u
.

To this end, as done in
[
3 (C-L)
]
, we employ a two-step approach:

•

Step 1:
(u_{n},R_{n})
is transformed into
(\overline{u}_{n},\overline{R}_{n})
by concentrating the stress tensor;

•

Step 2: space-time convex integration turning
(\overline{u}_{n},\overline{R}_{n})
into
(u{n+1},R_{n+1})
.

The first step is mainly to achieve a small singular set in time, and the second step ensures the convergences.

2.1
Step 1: Concentrating the stress error

Given
(u_{n-1},R_{n-1})
, we divide the time interval
[0,T]
into smaller sub-intervals
I_{i}
of length
\tau^{\epsilon}
, where
\tau>0
will be chosen to vary depending on
(u_{n-1},R_{n-1})
. The total number of sub-intervals is thus of order
\tau^{-\epsilon}
.

On each
I_{i}
, we solve a difference fractional Navier-Stokes system centered at
(u_{n-1},R_{n-1})
to obtain a corrector
v_{i}
on
I_{i}
. More precisely,
v_{i}:I_{i}\times\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
solves

\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\partial_{t}v_{i}-\Deltaup v_{i}+\operatorname{div}(v_{i}\otimes v_{i})+\operatorname{div}(v_{i}\otimes u)+\operatorname{div}(u\otimes v_{i})+\nabla q_{i}=\operatorname{div}R\\
\operatorname{div}v_{i}=0\\
v_{i}(t_{i})=0\end{array}\right.,

so that
u_{n-1}+v_{i}
is an exact solution of the fractional Navier-Stokes equations (
1.1
) on
I_{i}
.

To concentrate the error and obtain a solution of (
2
) on
[0,T]
, we apply a sharp cutoff
\chi_{i}
to the corrector
v_{i}
and obtain the glued solution
\overline{u}_{n-1}
defined by:

\overline{u}_{n-1}\coloneq u_{n-1}+\sum_{i}\chi_{i}v_{i}.

Specifically, each
\chi_{i}
equals 1 on a majority of
I_{i}
, but
\chi_{i}\equiv 0
near endpoint regionss of
I_{i}
of length of order
\tau
. Since
\epsilon\ll 1
, the cutoff
\chi_{i}
is very sharp compared to the length of the sub-interval
I_{i}
.

On one hand, due to the sharp cutoff
\chi_{i}
, the stress error
\overline{R}_{n-1}
associated with
\overline{u}_{n-1}
will only be supported on endpoint regions of
I_{i}
of length of order
\tau
. In other words, the temporal support of
\overline{u}_{n}
can be covered by
\sim\tau^{-\epsilon}
many intervals of size
\sim\tau
, from which one already sees the singular set of the final solution will have a small dimension.

On the other hand, the corrector
v_{i}
is very small, say in
L^{\infty}_{t}H^{d}_{x}
, since it starts with initial data 0 and we can choose time scale
\tau^{\epsilon}=|I_{i}|
to be sufficiently small. More importantly, the new stress error
\overline{R}_{n-1}
associated with
\overline{u}_{n-1}
satisfies the estimate:

{\left\|\overline{R}_{n-1}\right\|}_{L^{1}_{t}L^{r}_{x}}\lesssim{\left\|R_{n-1}\right\|}_{L^{1}_{t}L^{r}_{x}}\qquad 1<r<\infty,

with an implicit constant independent of the time scale
\tau>0
. In other words, concentrating the stress error
R_{n-1}
to
\overline{R}_{n-1}
cost a loss of a constant multiple when measuring in
L^{1}
norm in time.

2.2
Step 2: Space-time convex integration

The next step is to use a convex integration technique to reduce the size of
\overline{R}_{n-1}
by adding a perturbation
w_{n}
to
\overline{u}_{n-1}
to obtain a new solution
(u_{n},R_{n})
of (
2
). The perturbation
w_{n}
and the new stress
R_{n}
satisfy the equation

\operatorname{div}R_{n}=\operatorname{div}\overline{R}_{n-1}+\operatorname{div}(w_{n}\otimes w_{n})+\partial_{t}w_{n}-\Deltaup w_{n}+\operatorname{div}(\overline{u}_{n-1}\otimes w_{n})+\operatorname{div}(w_{n}\otimes\overline{u}_{n-1})+\nabla P_{n},

for a suitable pressure
P_{n}
. Heuristically, we wish to balance the old Reynolds stress with the quadratic term
w_{n}\otimes w_{n}
, that is:

\operatorname{div}(\overline{R}_{n-1}+w_{n}\otimes w_{n})=HSF+HTF+LO.

Here, HSF are terms of high spatial frequency, HTF have high temporal frequency and will be balanced further by a part of
\partial_{t}w_{n}
, and LO are lower-order terms. This is similar to
[
2 (B-V)
,
1 (B-C-V)
]
, but with the fundamental difference that this additional “convex integration in time” requires no additional oscillation/concentration constraint and is basically free, which is crucial in obtaining the regularity ranges
L^{p}_{t}L^{q}_{t}\cap L^{s}_{t}W^{\gamma,q}_{x}
of our main theorem.

In order to obtain such balances, we need two ingredients:

(1)

Suitable stationary flows as the spatial building blocks of our perturbation; these must be able to achieve some level of spatial concentration;

(2)

Intermittent temporal functions to oscillate the spatial building blocks in time.

Once the first ingredient is found, the second one is relatively straightforward to implement. On the technical side, the first ingredient will be the Mikado flows introduced in
[
4 (D-Sz)
]
. These are periodic pipe flows that can be arranged to be supported on periodic cylinders with small radius. In other words, Mikado flows can achieve
(d-1)
-dimensional concentration on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
. It is worth noting that, in the framework of
[
2 (B-V)
]
, stationary Mikado flows are not sufficiently intermittent to be used for the Navier-Stokes equations ((
1.1
) with
\theta=1
) in dimension
d\leq 3
.

The desired balance (
2.2
) imposes a relation between
w_{n}
and
\overline{R}_{n-1}
:

{\left\|w_{n}\right\|}_{L^{2}_{t,x}}\sim{\left\|\overline{R}_{n-1}\right\|}_{L^{1}_{t,x}}.

This relation will imply the
L^{2}_{t,x}
convergence of the approximate solutions
w_{n}
as long as one can successfully reduce the size of the stress error:

{\left\|R_{n}\right\|}_{L^{1}_{t,x}}\ll{\left\|\overline{R}_{n-1}\right\|}_{L^{1}_{t,x}}.

In particular, special attention will be paid to estimating the temporal derivative component of the new Reynolds stress, defined by

\operatorname{div}R_{tem}=\partial_{t}w_{n},

and achieving the regularity of the perturbation:

{\left\|w_{n}\right\|}_{L^{p}_{t}L^{q}_{x}}+{\left\|w_{n}\right\|}_{L^{s}_{t}W^{\gamma,q}_{x}}\ll 1.

These two constraints (
2.2
) and (
2.2
) require a very delicate parameter choice when designing the perturbation. On one hand, (
2.2
) implies the temporal frequency cannot bee too large, relative to the spatial frequency, otherwise the time derivative will dominate. On the other hand, (
2.2
) requires a large temporal frequency, so as to use the temporal concentration to offset the loss caused by going from
L^{2}
to
L^{q}
or
W^{\gamma,q}
in space in relation to (
2.2
). This is achieved by, roughly speaking, a tradeoff: we trade
L^{2}
in time, reducing it to
L^{p}
or
L^{s}
, in order to obtain
L^{q}
or
W^{\gamma,q}
in space.

2.3
Oscillation and concentration

We do this computation in general dimension
d\geq 2
and
D\!\in\![0,d]
denotes the spatial intermittency.

We start with a velocity perturbatoin in
L^{2}_{t,x}
with a certain decay given by the previous stress error:

{\left\|w_{n}\right\|}_{L^{2}_{t,x}}\lesssim 1.

Denote the spatial frequency by
\lambda
and the temporal frequency by
\kappa
, namely:

{\left\|\partial_{t}^{m}\nabla^{n}w_{n}\right\|}_{L^{2}_{t,x}}\lesssim\kappa^{m}\lambda^{n}.

The intermittency parameter
D
in space dictates the level of concentration of
w_{n}
and the scaling law:

{\left\|w_{n}(t)\right\|}_{L^{q}}\lesssim{\left\|w_{n}(t)\right\|}_{L^{2}}\lambda^{(d-D){\left(\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}-\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle q$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle q$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$q$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$q$}}$}}\right)}}.

As for the temporal scaling, we assume for simplicity that
w_{n}
is fully concentrated in time:

{\left\|w_{n}\right\|}_{L^{p}_{t}L^{q}_{x}}\lesssim{\left\|w_{n}\right\|}_{L^{2}_{t}L^{q}_{x}}\lambda^{\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}-\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle p$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle p$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$p$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$p$}}$}}}\sim\kappa^{\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}-\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle p$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle p$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$p$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$p$}}$}}}\lambda^{(d-D){\left(\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}-\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle q$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle q$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$q$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$q$}}$}}\right)}}.

With such scaling laws, we effectively assume negligible temporal oscillation and the goal then reduces to finding a choice of
D
in terms of the parameters
d,p,q
. In other words, we need to find a balance between spatial oscillation and spatial concentration.

By the scaling relations just above, the stress error contributed by the time derivative (
2.2
) satisfies:

{\left\|\operatorname{div}^{-1}\partial_{t}w_{n}\right\|}_{L^{1}_{t,x}}\lesssim\kappa^{\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}}\lambda^{-1}\lambda^{\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle d-D$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle d-D$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$d-D$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$d-D$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}},

where we assume
\operatorname{div}^{-1}
gains one full derivative in space. The regularity condition (
2.2
) then becomes:

\displaystyle{\left\|w_{n}\right\|}_{L^{p}_{t}L^{q}_{x}}\sim\kappa^{\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}-\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle p$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle p$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$p$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$p$}}$}}}\lambda^{\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle d-D$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle d-D$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$d-D$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$d-D$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}}\ll 1

\displaystyle{\left\|w_{n}\right\|}_{L^{s}_{t}W^{\gamma,q}_{x}}\sim\kappa^{\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}-\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$s$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$s$}}$}}}\lambda^{1+\gamma+\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle d-D$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle d-D$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$d-D$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$d-D$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}-\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle d-D$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle q$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle d-D$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle q$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$d-D$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$q$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$d-D$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$q$}}$}}}.

(2.7)

Conditions (
2.3
) and (
2.7
) imply that:

\kappa^{\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$s$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$s$}}$}}}\lambda^{1+\gamma+\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle d-D$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle d-D$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$d-D$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$d-D$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}-\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle d-D$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle q$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle d-D$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle q$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$d-D$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$q$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$d-D$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$q$}}$}}}\ll\kappa^{\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}}\ll\lambda^{1+\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle d-D$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle d-D$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$d-D$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$d-D$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}}.

Solutions to this exist for the ranges of parameters described in Section
6
.

2.4
The main iteration proposition

We are ready to introduce the main iteration proposition of the paper that materializes the above discussion. To simplify presentation, let us introduce the notion of well-prepared solutions to (
2
), which encodes the small Hausdorff dimension of the set of singular times. Throughout the paper, we take
T=1
and assume
0<\epsilon<1
without loss of generality.

Definition 2.4.1
(name=Well-prepared solution,label=thm:defi:WPS).

We say a smooth solution
(u,R)
of (
2
) on
[0,1]
is
well-prepared
if there exist a set
I
and a length scale
\tau>0
such that
I
is a union of at most
\tau^{-\epsilon}
-many closed intervals of length
5\tau
, and:

R(t,x)=0\qquad\forall t:\operatorname{dist}(t,I^{c})\leq\tau.

With this definition, to ensure the solution
u
has intervals of regularity with a small residue set of Hausdorff dimension
\lesssim\epsilon
, it suffices to construct approximate solutions
(u_{n},R_{n})
that are well-prepared for some
I_{n},\tau_{n}
such that:

I_{n}\!\subset\!I_{n-1}\qquad\tau_{n}\to 0.

The main proposition of this paper states as follows.

Proposition 2.4.1
(name=Main iteration,label=thm:propo:MI).

For any
\epsilon>0
, there exists a universal constant
M=M(\epsilon)>0
such that for any
p,q,s,\gamma,\theta
in the ranges of
(??)
and
d\geq 2
there exists
r=r(p,q,s,d,\gamma,\theta)>1
such that the following holds.

Let
\delta>0
and
(u,R)
be a well-prepared smooth solution of (
2
) for some set
\tilde{I}
and a length scale
\tilde{\tau}>0
. Then there exists another well-prepared smooth solution
(u_{1},R_{1})
of (
2
) for some set
I\!\subset\!\tilde{I}
with
0,1\!\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33334pt\kern-4.99998pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33334pt\kern-4.99998pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69444pt\kern-4.18054pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.68057pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}\!I
, and some time scale
\tau<{}^{\tilde{\tau}}\!/_{2}
, such that:

{\left\|R_{1}\right\|}_{L^{1}_{t}L^{r}_{x}}\leq\delta.

Moreover, the velocity perturbation
w\coloneq u_{1}-u
satisfies:

\displaystyle\operatorname{supp}w\!\subset\!{}

\displaystyle I\times\mathbb{T}^{d}

(2.8)

\displaystyle{\left\|w\right\|}_{L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{x}}\leq{}

\displaystyle M{\left\|\overline{R}\right\|}_{L^{1}_{t}L^{1}_{x}}

(2.9)

\displaystyle{\left\|w\right\|}_{L^{p}_{t}L^{q}_{x}}+{\left\|w\right\|}_{L^{s}_{t}W^{\gamma,q}_{x}}\leq{}

\displaystyle\delta.

(2.10)

A couple of comments to close this section:

•

The parameter
r>1
is used to ensure the
L^{r}
boundedness of the Calderón-Zygmund singular integral and is very close to 1;

•

Due to the local well-preparedness, on large portions of the time axis the solutions are exact solutions of the fractional Navier-Stokes equations (
1.1
), and we do not touch them in the future.

The main theorem
(??)
is deduced from this proposition with precisely the same arguments of
[
3 (C-L)
, Section 2.6, proof of Theorem 1.7]
.

3
Concentrating the stress error

The goal of this section is to prove
(??)
below. The idea is that, given a solution
(u,R)
of (
2
), we can add a small correction term to it so that all of the stress error
R
concentrates on a set
I
, the union of small intervals of length
\tau
, and thus obtain a new solution
(\overline{u},\overline{R})
. The key is that the procedure
(u,R)\leadsto(\overline{u},\overline{R})
leaves the size of the stress
R
invariant in
L^{1}_{t}
, up to a cost of a constant multiple:

{\left\|\overline{R}\right\|}_{L^{1}_{t}L^{r}_{x}}\leq C{\left\|R\right\|}_{L^{1}_{t}L^{r}_{x}}\qquad r\!\in\!(1,\infty),

where
C=C(r,\epsilon)
is a universal constant that only depends on the exponent
r
and the well-preparedness parameter
\epsilon>0
.

Proposition 3.0.1
(name=Error concentration,label=thm:propo:StressConc).

Let
0<\epsilon<1
and
(u,R)
be a well-prepared smooth solution of (
2
) for some set
\tilde{I}
and length scale
\tilde{\tau}>0
. For any
1<r<\infty
, there exists a universal constant
C=C(r,\epsilon)>0
such that the following holds.

For any
\delta>0
, there exists another well-prepared smooth solution
(\overline{u},\overline{R})
of (
2
), for some set
0,1\!\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33334pt\kern-4.99998pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33334pt\kern-4.99998pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69444pt\kern-4.18054pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.68057pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}\!I\!\subset\!\tilde{I}
and length scale
\tau<{}^{\tilde{\tau}}\!/_{2}
, satisfying the following:

(1)

The new stress error
\overline{R}
satisfies:

\displaystyle\overline{R}(t,x)={}

\displaystyle 0\qquad\operatorname{dist}(t,I^{C})\leq\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 3$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 3$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$3$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$3$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}\tau

\displaystyle{\left\|\overline{R}\right\|}_{L^{1}_{t}L^{r}_{x}([0,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})}\leq{}

\displaystyle C{\left\|R\right\|}_{L^{1}_{t}L^{r}_{x}([0,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})};

(2)

The velocity perturbation
\overline{w}\coloneq\overline{u}-u
satisfies:

\displaystyle\operatorname{supp}\overline{w}\!\subset\!{}

\displaystyle\tilde{I}\times\mathbb{T}^{d}

\displaystyle{\left\|\overline{w}\right\|}_{L^{\infty}_{t}H^{d}_{x}([0,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})}\leq\delta.

Note the slightly stricter bound
\operatorname{dist}(t,I^{c})\leq{}^{3}\!/_{2}\tau
versus the definition of well-preparedness is to leave room for the upcoming convex integration scheme in the next section.

3.1
Subdividing the time interval

We first introduce a subdivision of the time interval
[0,1]
. Then on each subinterval
[t_{i},t_{i+1}]
we solve a generalized fractional Navier-Stokes system and obtain a solution
v_{i}
so that
u+v_{i}
is an exact solution of the fractional Navier-Stokes equations (
1.1
) on
[t_{i},t_{i+1}]
.

Let
\tau>0
be a small length scale to be fixed at the end of this section, and define:

t_{i}\coloneq i\tau^{\epsilon}\qquad 0\leq u\leq\lfloor\tau^{-\epsilon}\rfloor.

Without loss of generality, we assume
\tau^{-\epsilon}
is always an integer so that the time interval
[0,1]
is perfectly divided.

For
0\leq i\leq\tau^{-\epsilon}-1
, let
v_{i}:[t_{i},t_{i+1}]\times\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
q_{u}:[t_{i},t_{i+1}]\times\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be the solution of the following system:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\partial_{t}v_{i}+(-\Deltaup)^{\theta}v_{i}+\operatorname{div}(v_{i}\otimes v_{i})+\operatorname{div}(v_{i}\otimes u+u\otimes v_{i})+\nabla q_{i}=-\operatorname{div}R\\
\operatorname{div}v_{i}=0\\
v_{i}(t_{i})=0\end{array}\right..

Since the initial data for
v_{i}
is zero and
u
and
R
are smooth on
[0,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
, thanks to the general local well-posedness theory for the fractional Navier-Stokes equations, for all sufficiently small
\tau>0
we may solve the above system on intervals
t\!\in\![t_{i},t_{i+1}]
to obtain a unique smooth solution
v_{i}
.

We shall focus on estimating each
v_{i}
on the associated interval
[t_{i},t_{i+1}]
. The solution
v_{i}
serves as an “accumulator” of the stress error on
[t_{i},t_{i+1}]
, and it will provide the major contribution to the new stress error
\overline{R}
once we use a gluing procedure.

Recall that
\mathcal{R}:\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{C}^{d\times d}_{0})
is an inverse divergence operator on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
defined in
[
3 (C-L)
, Appendix B]
. The below result quantifies the size of the corrector
v_{i}
in relation to the time scale
\tau
and the forcing
-\operatorname{div}R
.

Proposition 3.1.1
(name=,label=thm:propo:Estvizi).

Let
d\geq 2
and
(u,R)
be a smooth solution of (
2
). There exists a universal constant
C_{r}
depending on
1<r<\infty
so that the following holds.

For any
\delta>0
, if
\tau>0
is sufficiently small, then the unique smooth solution
v_{i}
to (
3.1
) on
[t_{i},t_{i+1}]
satisfies

\displaystyle{\left\|v_{i}\right\|}_{L^{\infty}_{t}H^{d}_{x}([t_{i},t_{i+1}]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})}\leq{}

\displaystyle\delta

\displaystyle{\left\|\mathcal{R}v_{i}\right\|}_{L^{\infty}_{t}L^{r}_{x}([t_{i},t_{i+1}]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})}\leq{}

\displaystyle C_{i}\int\limits_{t_{i}\mskip 4.0mu}^{\mskip 4.0mut_{i+1}}\hskip-2.0pt{\left\|R(t)\right\|}_{L^{r}}\mskip 0.0mu\mathrm{d}\mskip 0.5mut+C_{u}\delta\tau^{\epsilon},

where
C_{u}
is a sufficiently large constant depending on
u
but not
\delta
or
\tau
.

Proof.

The first estimate follows from a simple
H^{d}
energy estimate combined with Grönwall’s inequality.

To prove the second one, assume
\delta>0
is sufficiently small, and denote
v\coloneq v_{i},z\coloneq\mathcal{R}v
for brevity. Note that (
3.1
) preserves the zero-mean condition.

Let
\mathbb{P}
be the Leray projection onto divergence-free vector fields on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
. By projecting (
3.1
) with
\mathbb{P}
and applying
\mathcal{R}
, we find that the evolution of
z
is governed by

\partial_{t}z+(-\Deltaup)^{\theta}z=-\mathcal{R}\mathbb{P}\operatorname{div}(v\otimes v+u\otimes v+v\otimes u)-\mathcal{R}\mathbb{P}\operatorname{div}R.

In order to obtain an estimate in
L^{r}
, we multiply both sides by
z|z|^{r-2}
:

z\partial_{t}z|z|^{r-2}+z(-\Deltaup)^{\theta}z|z|^{r-2}=-\mathcal{R}\mathbb{P}\operatorname{div}(v\otimes v+v\otimes u+u\otimes v)z|z|^{r-2}-\mathcal{R}\mathbb{P}\operatorname{div}Rz|z|^{r-2}.

Integrating on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle r$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle r$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$r$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$r$}}$}}\partial_{t}{\left\|z\right\|}_{L^{r}}^{r}={}

\displaystyle-\int\limits_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\mskip 4.0mu}^{\mskip 4.0mu}\hskip-2.0pt((-\Deltaup)^{\theta}z)(z|z|^{r-2})\mskip 0.0mu\mathrm{d}\mskip 0.5mux+\int\limits_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\mskip 4.0mu}^{\mskip 4.0mu}\hskip-2.0pt\mathcal{R}\mathbb{P}\operatorname{div}Rz|z|^{r-2}\mskip 0.0mu\mathrm{d}\mskip 0.5mux

\displaystyle{}-\int\limits_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\mskip 4.0mu}^{\mskip 4.0mu}\mathcal{R}\mathbb{P}\operatorname{div}(v\otimes v+v\otimes u+u\otimes v)z|z|^{r-2}\mskip 0.0mu\mathrm{d}\mskip 0.5mux.

By applying Hölder’s inequality, this implies

{\left\|z\right\|}_{L^{r}}^{r-1}\partial_{t}{\left\|z\right\|}_{L^{r}}\leq{\left({\left\|(-\Deltaup)^{\theta}z\right\|}_{L^{r}}+{\left\|\mathcal{R}\mathbb{P}\operatorname{div}(v\otimes v+v\otimes u+u\otimes v\right\|}_{L^{r}}+{\left\|\mathcal{R}\mathbb{P}\operatorname{div}R\right\|}_{L^{r}}\right)}{\left\|z|z|^{r-2}\right\|}_{L^{\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle r$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle r-1$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle r$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle r-1$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$r$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$r-1$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$r$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$r-1$}}$}}}}.

Since
\|z|z|^{r-2}\|_{L^{\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle r$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle r-1$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle r$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle r-1$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$r$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$r-1$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$r$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$r-1$}}$}}}}=\|z\|_{L^{r}}^{r-1}
and
\mathcal{R}\mathbb{P}\operatorname{div}
is a Canderón-Zygmund operator, we conclude

\partial_{t}{\left\|z\right\|}_{L^{r}}\lesssim{\left\|(-\Deltaup)^{\theta}z\right\|}_{L^{r}}+{\left\|v\otimes v+v\otimes u+u\otimes v\right\|}_{L^{r}}+{\left\|R\right\|}_{L^{r}}.

Integrating over
[t_{i},t]
, we obtain

\displaystyle{\left\|z(t)\right\|}_{L^{r}}\lesssim{}

\displaystyle{\left\|z(t_{i})\right\|}_{L^{r}}+\int\limits_{t_{i}\mskip 4.0mu}^{\mskip 4.0mut}\hskip-2.0pt{\left\|R(s)\right\|}_{L^{r}}+{\left\|(-\Deltaup)^{\theta}z(s)\right\|}_{L^{r}}\mskip 0.0mu\mathrm{d}\mskip 0.5mus

\displaystyle{}+\int\limits_{t_{i}\mskip 4.0mu}^{\mskip 4.0mut}\hskip-2.0pt{\left\|(v\otimes v)(s)\right\|}_{L^{r}}+{\left\|(v\otimes u)(s)\right\|}_{L^{r}}+{\left\|(u\otimes v)(s)\right\|}_{L^{r}}\mskip 0.0mu\mathrm{d}\mskip 0.5mus.

z(t_{i})=\mathcal{R}(v(t_{i})\!)=0
. The last term is easily bounded by the first one in the desired estimate. All other terms must be estimated by
C_{u}\delta\tau^{\epsilon}
. Since they are integrated over an interval of length at most
\tau^{\epsilon}
, the following claim concludes the proof:

{\left\|v\otimes v\right\|}_{L^{r}}+{\left\|v\otimes u\right\|}_{L^{r}}+{\left\|u\otimes v\right\|}_{L^{r}}+{\left\|(-\Deltaup)^{\theta}z\right\|}_{L^{t}}\lesssim\delta,

for a.e.
s\!\in\![t_{i},t_{i+1}]
, where the constant can depend on
u
. Since
u
is smooth,
\|u\|_{L^{\infty}L^{\infty}}
is finite, so

{\left\|v\otimes u\right\|}_{L^{r}}+{\left\|u\otimes v\right\|}_{L^{r}}\leq{\left\|u\right\|}_{L^{\infty}L^{\infty}}{\left\|v\right\|}_{L^{\infty}L^{r}}\leq{\left\|u\right\|}_{L^{\infty}L^{\infty}}{\left\|v\right\|}_{L^{\infty}H^{d}}\lesssim\delta.

Analogously

{\left\|v\otimes v\right\|}_{L^{r}}\leq{\left\|v\right\|}_{L^{\infty}L^{\infty}}{\left\|v\right\|}_{L^{\infty}L^{r}}\leq{\left\|v\right\|}_{L^{\infty}H^{d}}^{2}\leq\delta^{2}\leq\delta,

provided
\delta\leq 1
. Coming to the fractional laplacian term, define

I(\theta)\coloneq\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}\lfloor 2\theta\rfloor\qquad D(\theta)\coloneq\theta-I(\theta).

We can then write:

\displaystyle{\left\|(-\Deltaup)^{\theta}z\right\|}_{L^{r}}^{2}\lesssim{}

\displaystyle{\left\|(-\Deltaup)^{\theta}v\right\|}_{L^{2}}^{2}={\left\|(-\Deltaup)^{I(\theta)}[(-\Deltaup)^{D(\theta)}v]\right\|}_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle{}={}

\displaystyle\mskip-26.0mu\sum_{i_{1},\dotsc,i_{I(\theta)}=1}^{d}\mskip-4.0mu{\left\|\partial_{i_{1}}^{2}\dotso\partial_{i_{I(\theta)}}^{2}(-\Deltaup)^{D(\theta)}v\right\|}_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle{}\lesssim{}

\displaystyle\mskip-26.0mu\sum_{i_{1},\dotsc,i_{I(\theta)}=1}^{d}\mskip-4.0mu{\left|\partial_{i_{1}}^{2}\dotso\partial_{i_{I(\theta)}}^{2}v\right|}_{\dot{H}^{2D(\theta)}}^{2}\leq{\left\|v\right\|}_{H^{2\theta}}^{2}.

Here, we have used:

•

The fact
r\leq 2
and thus, since
\mathbb{T}^{d}
is of finite measure,
L^{2}\hookrightarrow L^{r}
, and that
\mathcal{R}:L^{r}\to L^{r}
is bounded;

•

A composition property of fractional laplacians;

•

The definition of integer laplacian;

•

A combination of
[
5 (DN-P-V)
, Proposition 3.3, p. 14]
and
[
5 (DN-P-V)
, Proposition 3.6, p. 18]
.

By
[
5 (DN-P-V)
, Proposition 2.1, p. 6]
, assuming
2\theta\leq d
, we have:

{\left\|(-\Deltaup)^{\theta}z\right\|}_{L^{2}}\leq{\left\|v\right\|}_{H^{d}},

which we have already estimated by
C_{u}\delta
. This completes the proof.
\Diamondx

3.2
Temporal concentration by sharp gluing

Since
u+v_{i}
is an exact solution of the fractional Navier-Stokes equations (
1.1
) on each interval
[t_{i},t_{i+1}]
for
0\leq i\leq\tau^{-\epsilon}-1
, the next step is a suitable gluing of the
v_{i}
so that the glued solution
u+\sum_{i}\chi_{i}v_{i}
is still an exact solution on a majority of the time interval
[0,1]
, with an error supported on many disjoint sub-intervals.

We first choose cutoff functions to glue the
v_{i}
together. We define
\chi_{i}\!\in\!\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
to be a smooth cutoff such that, for
1\leq i\leq\tau^{-\epsilon}-2
,

\chi_{i}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{\,}cl@{}}1&t_{i}+\tau\leq t\leq t_{i+1}-\tau\\
0&t_{i}+\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\tau$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle\tau$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\tau$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\tau$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}\geq t\text{ or }t\geq t_{i+1}-\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\tau$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle\tau$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\tau$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\tau$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}\end{array}\right.,

and for
i\!\in\!\{0,\tau^{-\epsilon}-1\}

\displaystyle\chi_{0}={}

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{\,}cl@{}}1&0\leq t\leq t_{1}-\tau\\
0&t\geq t_{1}-\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\tau$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle\tau$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\tau$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\tau$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}\end{array}\right.,

\displaystyle\chi_{\tau^{-\epsilon}-1}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{\,}cl@{}}1&t_{\tau^{-\epsilon}-1}+\tau\leq t\leq 1\\
0&t\leq t_{\tau^{-\epsilon}}+\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\tau$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle\tau$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\tau$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\tau$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}\end{array}\right..

(3.3)

In other words, we do not cut near the endpoints
t=0,t=1
, and the glued solution
\overline{u}
is an exact solution near
t=0
and
t=1
. It is worth noting that in the iteration scheme
v_{i}
for
i=0,i=\tau^{-\epsilon}-1
will be zero after step 1, since it already solves (
2
) exactly there, and thus the above properties of
\chi_{0},\chi_{\tau^{-\epsilon}-1}
are only used once.

Furthermore, we require the following bounds uniformly in
\tau,i
:

{\left|\nabla^{m}\chi_{i}\right|}\lesssim_{m}\tau^{-m}.

Note that for sub-intervals
[t_{i},t_{i+1}]
, with
1\leq i\leq\tau^{-\epsilon}-2
, we cut near both the left and the right endpoint. The left cutoff is to ensure smoothness near
t_{i}
, since each
v_{i}
only has a limited amount of time regularity at
t=t_{i}
, whereas the right cutoff is where the gluing will take place. With
\chi_{i}
in hand, we can simply define the glued solution:

\overline{u}\coloneq u+\sum_{i}\chi_{i}v_{i}=u+\overline{w}.

It is clear that
\overline{u}:[0,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is spatially mean-free and solenoidal. It remains to show that
\overline{u}
satisfies the properties listed in
(??)
.

Heuristically,
\overline{u}
should be an exact solution with a stress error supported on smaller intervals of size
\tau
. To confirm this claim, we must computer the stress error
\overline{R}
associated with
\overline{u}
. Since the
\chi_{i}
are disjointly supported, we can compute

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\overline{u}+(-\Deltaup)^{\theta}\overline{u}={}

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}R+(\partial_{t}+(-\Deltaup)^{\theta})\sum_{i}\chi_{i}v_{i}

\displaystyle{}+\sum_{i}\chi_{i}\operatorname{div}(u\otimes v_{i})+\sum_{i}\chi_{i}\operatorname{div}(v_{i}\otimes u)

\displaystyle{}+\sum_{i}\chi_{i}^{2}\operatorname{div}(v_{i}\otimes v_{i}).

Thus, using the fact that
v_{i}
solves (
3.1
) on
[t_{i},t_{i+1}]
and
u
solves (
2
) on
[0,1]
, we have:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\overline{u}+(-\Deltaup)^{\theta}u+\operatorname{div}(\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u})+\nabla p={}

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}R+\sum_{i}\partial_{t}\chi_{i}v_{i}+\sum_{i}(\chi_{i}^{2}-\chi_{i})\operatorname{div}(v_{i}\otimes v_{i})

\displaystyle{}+\sum_{i}\chi_{i}(\partial_{t}v_{i}-(-\Deltaup)^{\theta}v_{i}+\operatorname{div}(\!(u+v_{i})\otimes v_{i})+\operatorname{div}(u_{i}\otimes u)\!)

\displaystyle{}={}

\displaystyle{\left(1-\sum_{i}\chi_{i}\right)}\operatorname{div}R+\sum_{i}\partial_{t}\chi_{i}v_{i}

\displaystyle{}+\sum_{i}(\chi_{i}^{2}-\chi_{i})\operatorname{div}(v_{i}\otimes v_{i})-\sum_{i}\chi_{i}\nabla q_{i}.

Now let

\overline{R}\coloneq{\left(1-\sum\chi_{i}\right)}R+\mathcal{R}\sum_{i}\partial_{t}\chi_{i}v_{i}+\sum_{i}(\chi_{i}^{2}-\chi_{i})v_{i}\mathbin{\mathaccent 23{\otimes}}v_{i},

where
\mathaccent 23{\otimes}
denotes a traceless tensor produce, i.e.
f\mathbin{\mathaccent 23{\otimes}}g=f_{i}g_{j}-\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle d$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle d$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$d$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$d$}}$}}\delta_{ij}f_{k}g_{k}
. Since each
v_{i}
has zero spatial mean,
\operatorname{div}\mathcal{R}v_{i}=v_{i}
, and we can conclude that

\partial_{t}\overline{u}-(-\Deltaup)^{\theta}\overline{u}+\operatorname{div}(\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u})+\nabla\overline{p}=\operatorname{duv}\overline{R},

where the pressure
\overline{p}:[0,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is defined by:

\overline{p}=p+\sum_{i}\chi_{i}q_{i}-\sum_{i}(\chi_{i}^{2}-\chi)\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle|v_{i}|^{2}$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle d$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle|v_{i}|^{2}$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle d$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$|v_{i}|^{2}$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$d$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$|v_{i}|^{2}$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$d$}}$}}.

The last step is then to show that the new Reynolds stress
\overline{R}
is comparable to the original one in the
L^{1}_{t}L^{r}
norm. It is clear that
\overline{R}
is much more “turbulent” than the original
R
as its value changes much more drastically due to the sharp cutoffs near the endpoints of each
[t_{i},t_{i+1}]
.

The heuristic is that, if
\tau
is small enough, then
v_{i}
linearly with a rate of order
\operatorname{div}R
, and therefore gluing the
v_{i}
together only counts the input from the stress forcing
\operatorname{div}R
. More precisely, the leading order term in (
3.2
) is the second term, where
\mathcal{R}v_{i}
is proportional to
R
thanks to
(??)
.

Proposition 3.2.1
.

For any
1<r<\infty
, there exists a universal constant
C_{r}
depending on
r
and
\epsilon
such that for all sufficiently small
\tau>0
the glue solution
(\overline{u},\overline{R})
safistifs

{\left\|\overline{R}\right\|}_{L^{1}_{t}L^{r}_{x}}\leq C_{r}{\left\|R\right\|}_{L^{1}_{t}L^{r}_{x}}.

The proof of this is left to
[
3 (C-L)
]
, where it is Proposition 3.3 on p. 16.

3.3
Proof of
(??)

We conclude this section with the last step in the proof of
(??)
. Since all the estimates have been obtained, we only need to verify that the temporal support of
\overline{w}=\sum_{i}\chi_{i}v_{i}
is contained in
\tilde{I}
, and that
(\overline{u},\overline{R})
is well-prepared.

Note that
(u,R)
is well-prepared for
\tilde{I},\tilde{\tau}
, and it follows from (
3.1
) that

v_{i}\equiv 0\qquad 0\leq i\leq\tau^{-\epsilon}\wedge R|_{[t_{i},t_{i+1}]}\equiv 0.

Hence, if
\tau^{\epsilon}=|[t_{i},t_{i+1}]|
is sufficiently smaller than
\tilde{\tau}
, the definition of well-preparedness of
(u,R)
implies that

\bigcup_{i}\operatorname{supp}_{t}(\chi_{i}v_{i})\!\subset\!\tilde{I}.

Thus we have proved that
\operatorname{supp}_{t}\overline{w}\!\subset\!\tilde{I}
.

Let us now show the well-preparedness of
(\overline{u},\overline{R})
. Define an index set

E\coloneq{\left\{i\!\in\!\mathbb{Z}:1\leq i\leq\tau^{-\epsilon}-1,v_{i}\not\equiv 0\right\}},

satisfying a trivial estimate:

{\left|E\right|}\leq\tau^{-\epsilon}.

The idea is that the concentrated stress
\overline{R}
is supported around each
t_{i}
for
i\!\in\!E
. Therefore, we can define a set on the time axis

I\coloneq\bigcup_{i\in E}{\left[t_{i}-\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 5$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 5$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$5$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$5$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}\tau,t_{i}+\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 5$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 5$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$5$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$5$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}\tau\right]},

where as before
t_{i}\coloneq i\tau^{\epsilon}
. Note that each interval in
I
has length
5\tau
and the total number of intervals is at most
\tau^{-\epsilon}
, consistent with the well-preparedness, and
0,1\!\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33334pt\kern-4.99998pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33334pt\kern-4.99998pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69444pt\kern-4.18054pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.68057pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\in}}}\!I
due to (
3.3
).

Now take any
t\!\in\![0,1]
such that
\operatorname{dist}(t,I^{c})\leq\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 3$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 3$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$3$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$3$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}\tau
. Then by (
3.2
) and (
3.3
)

\sum_{i}\chi_{i}(t)=1.

Moreover,
\partial_{t}\chi_{i}(t)=0
and
\chi_{i}(t)\!\in\!\{0,1\}
for any
i
. Consequently,

\overline{R}(t)={\left(1-\sum_{i}\chi_{i}\right)}R+\mathcal{R}\sum_{i}\partial_{t}\chi_{i}v_{i}+\sum_{i}(\chi_{i}^{2}-\chi_{i})v_{i}\mathbin{\mathaccent 23{\otimes}}v_{i}=0,

for every
t
such that
\operatorname{dist}(t,I^{c})\leq\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 3$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 3$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$3$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$3$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}\tau
. In particular,
(\overline{u},\overline{R})
is also well-prepared, which concludes the proof.

Remark 3.3.1
(name=First relations).

You may have noted that we obtained an estimate for
v
in
L^{\infty}_{t}H^{d}_{x}
, but our meta-theorem requires estimates in
L^{p}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}\cap L^{s}_{t}W^{\gamma,q}_{x}
. Now,
L^{\infty}_{t}H^{d}_{x}\hookrightarrow L^{p}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}
for any
p\geq 1
since we are on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
, but the embedding
L^{\infty}_{t}H^{d}_{x}\hookrightarrow L^{s}_{t}W^{\gamma,q}_{x}
is not guaranteed, since the embedding
H^{d}_{x}\hookrightarrow W^{\gamma,q}_{x}
is not. It is clear that, if
\gamma\leq d
and
q\leq 2
, or if
\gamma\leq\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle d$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle d$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$d$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$d$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}
, the embedding holds. By fractional Sobolev embedding, this will hold if:

q\leq\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2d$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2\gamma-d$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2d$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2\gamma-d$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$2d$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2\gamma-d$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$2d$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2\gamma-d$}}$}}\quad\text{or}\quad 2\gamma\leq d.

We can abbreviate this by writing:

\gamma\leq d\wedge q\leq\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2d$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle(2\gamma-d)^{+}$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2d$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle(2\gamma-d)^{+}$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$2d$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$(2\gamma-d)^{+}$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$2d$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$(2\gamma-d)^{+}$}}$}},

where “<1/0” is understood to mean “arbitrary”.

4
Convex integration in space-time

In this section, we will use a convex integration scheme to reduce the size of the Reynolds stress. The goal is to design a suitable velocity perturbation
w
to the glued solution
(\overline{u},\overline{R})
so that
u_{1}\coloneq\overline{u}+w
solves (
2
) with a much smaller Reynolds stress
R_{1}
.

The main goal of the current and the following section is summarized in the following proposition.

Proposition 4.1
(name=Main Perturbation Step,label=thm:propo:MPS).

There exists a universal constant
M>0
such that for any
p,q,s,\gamma,\theta
in the ranges of the main theorem and
d\geq 3
there exists
r=r(p,q,s,d)>1
such that the following holds.

Let
\delta>0
and
(\overline{u},\overline{R})
be a well-prepared smooth solution of (
2
). Then there exists another well-prepared smooth solution
(u_{1},R_{1})
of (
2
) for the same set
I
and time scale
\tau
such that:

{\left\|R_{1}\right\|}_{L^{1}_{t}L^{r}_{x}}\leq\delta.

Moreover, the velocity perturbation
w\coloneq u_{1}-u
satisfies:

\displaystyle\operatorname{supp}w\!\subset\!{}

\displaystyle I\times\mathbb{T}^{d}

(4.1)

\displaystyle{\left\|w\right\|}_{L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{x}}\leq{}

\displaystyle M{\left\|\overline{R}\right\|}_{L^{1}_{t}L^{1}_{x}}

(4.2)

\displaystyle{\left\|w\right\|}_{L^{p}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}+{\left\|w\right\|}_{L^{s}_{t}W^{\gamma,q}_{x}}\leq{}

\displaystyle\delta.

(4.3)

In the remainder of this section, we will construct the velocity perturbation
w
and define its associated Reynolds stress
R_{1}
and pressure
p_{1}
. The well-preparedness of
(u_{1},R_{1})
will be an easy consequence of the definition of
w
, whereas all the estimates will be proven in the next section.

4.1
Stationary Mikado flows for the convex integration

The main building blocks of the convex integration scheme are the Mikado flows
W_{k}:\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
introduced in
[
4 (D-Sz)
]
. In other contexts, they are called concentrated Mikado flows, or Mikado flows with concentration since they are supported on periodic cylinders with a small radius. Here for brevity, we refer to them as the Mikado flows.

We start with a geometric lemma that dates back to the work of Nash and is the key reason to use Mikado flows in convex integration schemes.
\mathcal{S}^{d\times d}_{+}
is the set of positive definite
d\times d
matrices and
e_{k}\coloneq\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle k$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle|k|$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle k$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle|k|$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$k$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$|k|$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$k$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$|k|$}}$}}
for all
k\!\in\!\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.

Lemma 4.1.1
(name=Decomposition lemma,label=thm:lemma:DecLemma).

For any compact subset
\mathcal{N}\!\subset\!\mathcal{S}^{d\times d}_{+}
, there exist a finite set
\Lambdaup\!\subset\!\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and smooth functions
\Gammaup_{k}\!\in\!\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{N};\mathbb{R})
for any
k\!\in\!\Lambdaup
such that:

R=\sum_{k\in\Lambdaup}\Gammaup_{k}^{2}(R)e_{k}\otimes e_{k}\qquad\forall R\!\in\!\mathcal{N}.

We apply this lemma for
\mathcal{N}=B_{\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}}(\operatorname{Id})
(the metric ball of radius
\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}
around the identity
\operatorname{Id}
in the space
\mathcal{S}^{d\times d}_{+}
) to obtain smooth functions
\Gammaup_{k}
for
k\!\in\!\Lambdaup\!\subset\!\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. Throughout this note, the direction set
\Lambdaup
is fixed, and we construct the Mikado flows as follows.

We choose points
p_{k}\!\in\!(0,1)^{d}
such that
p_{k}\neq p_{-k}
if both
k,-k\!\in\!\Lambdaup
. For each
k\!\in\!\Lambdaup
, we denote by
\ell_{k}\!\subset\!\mathbb{T}^{d}
the periodic line passing through
p_{k}
in direction
k
, namely:

\ell_{k}\coloneq{\left\{tk+p_{k}\!\in\!\mathbb{T}^{d}:t\!\in\!\mathbb{R}\right\}}.

Since
\Lambdaup
is a finite lattice set and we identify
\mathbb{T}^{d}
with a periodic box
[0,1]^{d}
, there exists a geometric constant
C_{\Lambdaup}\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
depending on the set
\Lambdaup
such that:

{\left|\ell_{k}\cap\ell_{k^{\prime}}\right|}\leq C_{\Lambdaup}\qquad\forall k,k^{\prime}\!\in\!\Lambdaup,

where we note that
\ell_{k}\cap\ell_{-k}=\varnothing
due to
p_{k}\neq p_{-k}
.
Let
\mu>0
be the spatial concentration parameter whose value will be fixed in the end of the proof. Let
\phi,\psi\!\in\!\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{c}([\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}},1])
be such that, if we define
{}_{k},\phi_{k}:\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
by:

\displaystyle{}_{k}\coloneq{}

\displaystyle\mu^{\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$d-1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$d-1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}}\psi(\mu\operatorname{dist}(\ell_{k},x)\!)

\displaystyle\phi_{k}\coloneq{}

\displaystyle\mu^{\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$d-1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$d-1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}-2}\phi(\mu\operatorname{dist}(\ell_{k},x)\!),

then:

\displaystyle\Deltaup\phi_{k}={}

\displaystyle{}_{k}\quad\text{on }\mathbb{T}^{d}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathop{}\mkern-2.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.27777pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.27777pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.56596pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.27084pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-2.0mu\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}{}_{k}^{2}\mskip 0.0mu\mathrm{d}\mskip 0.5mux=1.

Note that:

\displaystyle\operatorname{supp}{}_{k}\cap\operatorname{supp}{}_{k^{\prime}}\!\subset\!{}

\displaystyle{\left\{x\!\in\!\mathbb{T}^{d}:\operatorname{dist}(x,\ell_{k}\cap\ell_{k^{\prime}})\leq M_{\Lambdaup}\mu^{-1}\right\}},

for a sufficiently large constant
M_{\Lambdaup}
depending on
\Lambdaup
.

Finally, the stationary Mikado flows
W_{k}:\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
are defined by:

W_{k}\coloneq{}_{k}e_{k},

where the constant vector
e_{k}=\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle k$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle|k|$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle k$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle|k|$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$k$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$|k|$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$k$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$|k|$}}$}}
. Using the gradient field
\nabla\phi_{k}
, we may write
W_{k}
as a divergence of a skew-symmetric tensor
{}_{k}\!\in\!\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
:

{}_{k}\coloneq e_{k}\otimes\nabla\phi_{k}-\nabla\phi_{k}\otimes e_{k}.

Indeed,
k
is a skew-symmetric tensor by definition, and by a direct computation:

\operatorname{div}{}_{k}=\operatorname{div}(\nabla\phi_{k})e_{k}-(e_{k}\cdot\nabla)\nabla\phi_{k}=\Deltaup\phi_{k}e_{k}-0=W_{k}.

We summarize the main properties of the Mikado flows
W_{k}
in the following theorem.

Theorem 4.1.1
(name=Properties of the Mikado flows,label=thm:teor:PropMikFlows).

Let
d\geq 2
be the dimension. The stationary Mikado flows
W_{k}:\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfy the following.

(1)

Each
W_{k}\!\in\!\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
is divergence-free, satisfies:

W_{k}=\operatorname{div}{}_{k},

and solves the pressureless Euler equations:

\operatorname{div}(W_{k}\otimes W_{k})=0;

(2)

For any
1\leq p\leq\infty
, the following estimates hold uniformly in
\mu
:

\mu^{-m}{\left\|\nabla^{m}W_{k}\right\|}_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\lesssim_{m}\mu^{\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$d-1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$d-1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}-\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle p$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle p$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$d-1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$p$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$d-1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$p$}}$}}}\qquad\mu^{-m}{\left\|\nabla^{m}{}_{k}\right\|}_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\lesssim\mu^{-1+\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$d-1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$d-1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$2$}}$}}-\mathchoice{\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\displaystyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle p$}}}{\frac{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle p$}}}{\scalebox{0.5}[0.5]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$d-1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$p$}}$}}{\scalebox{0.4}[0.4]{$\dfrac{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$d-1$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$p$}}$}}};

(3)

For any
k\!\in\!\Lambdaup
, there holds:
